## Page 1


Nonlinear stability of continuously self-similar naked singularities
for the Einstein-scalar field equations II: linearized stability
Jaydeep Singh1 and Weihao Zheng∗2
1Department of Mathematics, Princeton University, Washington Road, Princeton, NJ, USA
2Department of Mathematics, Rutgers University, Hill Center, 110 Frelinghuysen Road, Piscataway, NJ, USA
May 18, 2026
Abstract
This is the second part of a series of papers proving the nonlinear stability of a one-parameter family
of continuous self-similar C1,α naked singularity solutions, with 0 < α ≪1, to the spherically symmetric
Einstein-scalar field equations. These solutions were constructed by Christodoulou and are known to be
unstable under sufficiently rough perturbations due to the blue-shift instability mechanism. In complete
contrast to the previous instability results, we establish the linearized stability for those naked singularity
spacetimes under perturbations of the same regularity as the background, revealing the central role of
regularity in determining the strength of the blue-shift instability mechanism, and showing that it is not
triggered at the regularity level of the background spacetime.
The linear analysis carried out in this paper provides the foundation for the nonlinear stability result
established in the companion paper [W. Zheng, Nonlinear stability of the continuous self-similar naked
singularities for the Einstein-scalar field equations I: main results]. Together with that companion paper,
this yields the nonlinear stability of these continuously self-similar naked singularities.

Introduction
In this paper and the companion paper [12], we prove the nonlinear stability of the k-self-similar naked
singularity solution to the Einstein-scalar field equations, which are a geometric system of PDEs describing
a Lorentzian manifold (M, g) coupled to a real scalar field ϕ : M →R
Ric[g]µν = ∂µϕ∂νϕ,
(1.1)
2gϕ = 0,
(1.2)
where Ric is the standard Ricci curvature of (M, g) and 2g is the covariant wave operator on (M, g). This
paper focuses on the linearized stability of these k-self-similar naked singularity solutions.
The family of k-self-similar naked singularity solutions (Mk, gk, ϕk) with 0 < k2 < 1/3, first constructed
by Christodoulou [3], is a family of spherically symmetric spacetimes solving (1.1)–(1.2) that contain a
curvature singularity without an event horizon and possess a complete future null infinity. Notably, the
regularity class of the initial data of (Mk, gk, ϕk) is not smooth. To be more precise, let O be the naked
singularity in Mk, C−
o be the past light cone emanating from the singularity, and Cout the initial null
hypersurface emanating from the center {r = 0}. The causal future of C−
o is called the exterior region of the

These k-self-similar naked singularity spacetimes (Mk, gk) have been proved to be unstable to trapped
surface formation under initial perturbations that are rougher than the background [4, 8, 7, 6, 1], while
they are stable for spherically symmetric perturbations supported in the exterior region of Cout at the same
regularity level as the background [9, 10, 11]. To obtain a complete understanding of their stability properties,
it is therefore sufficient to analyze how the interior region of (Mk, gk, ϕk) responds to initial perturbations
that are nontrivial within the interior of Cout1.
It has been shown that solutions to the linear wave equation on the interior of (Mk, gk, ϕk) are stable
under perturbations of the same regularity as the background [10]. As a next step toward nonlinear stability
for general perturbations at this regularity level, we therefore consider the linearized spherically symmetric
Einstein-scalar field equations in the present work.
Before we state the rough version of our main theorem, we define the function space we shall consider in
this paper. We parametrize the interior region of Cout by v ∈[−1, 0], where v = −1 corresponds to r = 0,
while v = 0 corresponds to Cout ∩C−

field equations
Although stability has been established for the linear wave equation on (Mk, gk) under
initial perturbations in C1/(1−k2),δ
N
[10], this is insufficient to justify expectations of nonlinear stability. The
argument in [10] crucially relies on the regularity of the background geometry gk, which is one derivative
more regular than the background scalar field ϕk. However, the Einstein-scalar field equations linearized
around (Mk, gk, ϕk) capture both the contribution of the background scalar field ϕk and its coupling with
the geometry gk. The presence of these effects introduces substantial analytical difficulties; see Section 1.2,
Section 3.3, and Section 3.4 for further discussions.
Nonlinear stability result in the companion paper [12]
In the companion paper [12], we use the
linearized result established here to further prove the following nonlinear stability of (Mk, gk, ϕk) under
small perturbations of the same regularity as ϕk|Cout, thereby falsifying the Weak Cosmic Censorship
conjecture for initial data of this regularity and showing the importance of the regularity class of the
initial data in the formulation of the Weak Cosmic Censorship conjecture.

1.1

One should note that the exact k-self-similar solution to the Einstein-scalar field equations is not asymptot-
ically flat. In [3], Christodoulou truncated the exact k-self-similar spacetime in the far-away region r ≫1 to
make it asymptotically flat. Nonetheless, we still call the asymptotically flat naked singularity spacetimes
in [3] the k-self-similar naked singularity spacetimes.
1.2
Previous works on the interior perturbations
The first instability result for the k-self-similar naked singularity spacetime (Mk, gk, ϕk) was proved in the
pioneering work of Christodoulou [4]. Subsequently, the stability and instability of the k-self-similar naked
singularities under perturbations supported in the exterior region have been extensively studied [8, 7, 1, 9, 10].
More precisely, the works [8, 10] proved the instability of (Mk, gk, ϕk) to trapped surface formation under
exterior spherical perturbations of regularity C1,α with α < k2/(1 −k2); the work [10] established stability
under exterior spherical perturbations of regularity C1,α with α ≥k2/(1 −k2); and [7, 1] proved instability
under exterior perturbations that are non-spherically symmetric and rougher than the background spacetime.
See Section 1.3 of the companion paper [12] for a more detailed review of these results.
Moreover, the mechanism for those low-regularity instability results [4, 8, 7, 1, 10] is called the blue-shift
instability mechanism, while the mechanism for those stability results [9, 10] is called the high-regularity
stabilizing effect; see Section 1.3 for a more detailed discussion of these mechanisms.
Motivated by the
5


## Page 6


transition from instability to stability as one increases the regularity of the initial perturbations, we refer to
the regularity of ϕk|Cout as the threshold regularity.
As mentioned above, to fully understand the stability aspect of the k-self-similar naked singularity, one
must consider general perturbations rather than the initial perturbations supported in the exterior region
considered in [9, 10].
Since the approach in [9, 10] for the exterior perturbations is robust enough to
be generalized to any naked singularity spacetime that is asymptotically k-self-similar, the key difficulty
in studying (in)stability under general perturbations lies in the study of the interior region under such
perturbations.
The study of the (in)stability of the k-self-similar naked singularity spacetime was initiated by the first
author in [10], where the linear wave equation
rk2gkϕ = rk∂u∂vϕ + ∂urk∂vϕ + ∂vrk∂uϕ = 0
(1.5)
on (Mk, gk) is considered for 0 < k2 ≪1. At the level of the linear wave equation, stability is understood
in terms of an asymptotic rate relative to the self-similar rate when approaching the singularity O. More
precisely, the scalar field ϕk in the k-self-similar naked singularity spacetime has the following bounds2

1
∂urk
∂uϕk
 ≲|u|−1 ,

1
∂vrk
∂vϕk
 ≲|u|−1 .
(1.6)
We refer to Section 2.2 for a detailed introduction to the k-self-similar spacetime.
The solutions to the linear wave equation 2gkϕ = 0 are said to be stable if the upper bounds of ∂uϕ/∂urk
and ∂vϕ/∂vrk are comparable to, or polynomially faster than the corresponding self-similar rates (1.6),
and unstable if they have polynomially slower rates than (1.6). It is shown in [10] that solutions arising
from interior perturbations of regularity below the threshold exhibit instability, whereas perturbations with
regularity at or above the threshold lead to stability. This instability aspect was subsequently established
at the nonlinear level in [6], where Li proved that, for interior perturbations below the threshold, solutions
to the Einstein-scalar field equations contain a trapped surface.
However, the stability result for the wave equation on the k-self-similar naked singularity interior does
not suffice to justify the expectation of linearized stability for the following two reasons:
• As seen from (1.4) and (1.5), the spherically symmetric linear wave equation depends only on the
background area radius rk, which is C2,k2/(1−k2) and hence one derivative more regular than the
background scalar field ϕk. Since the analysis in [10] crucially relies on the C1,k2/(1−k2) regularity of
∂rk, the linear wave equation avoids the lowest-regularity terms coming from the background scalar
field. In contrast, the linearized Einstein–scalar field equations contain the contribution of ϕk, making
the stability problem more delicate. More precisely, if one writes the spherically symmetric Einstein–
scalar field equations under the double-null gauge (1.3) as three wave equations for r, Ω2, and ϕ, then
the wave equation for Ω2 involves the quadratic term ∂ϕ · ∂ϕ. Upon linearization around the k-self-
similar spacetime, this produces a term of regularity C0,k2/(1−k2) in the wave equation for Ω2, which
falls outside the framework developed for the linear wave equation in [10].
• As we will review in detail in Section 3.3, the approach in [10] to proving stability for the linear wave
equation is to take the Fourier–Laplace transform of the corresponding wave equation and analyze the
distribution of the scattering resonances. A key step in establishing stability is to rule out exponentially
growing modes, except for those generated by the symmetries of the original equations. However, for
the linearized Einstein–scalar field equations, the coupling between the background geometry and the
background scalar field may produce additional nontrivial exponentially growing modes. The presence
of such modes would lead to a further instability mechanism; see Section 3.4.3 for a detailed discussion.
1.3
Blue-shift instability mechanism, high-regularity stabilizing effect, and model
problems for (in)stability
As we have mentioned above, the k-self-similar naked singularity spacetime is nonlinearly unstable under
perturbations below the threshold [4, 8, 7, 1, 6] and is nonlinearly stable under exterior perturbations above
2To avoid the confusion from the gauge choice, we state the bound for the gauge invariant quantities
1
∂urk ∂uϕk and
1
∂vrk ∂vϕk.
6


## Page 7


the threshold [9, 10]. The instability is driven by the so-called blue-shift instability mechanism, whereas
a stabilizing effect emerges for initial perturbations of high regularity. In this section, we illustrate these
mechanisms and describe a transition between the low-regularity instability and high-regularity stability
regimes by considering the behavior of linear waves on the exterior region and the interior region of k-
self-similar naked singularity spacetimes, respectively. These two model problems provide insight into the
complex relationship among the support and regularity of the initial perturbation, the regularity of the
background spacetime, and the leading order asymptotics of the solution.
Let φ(u, v) denote a spherically symmetric solution to the linear wave equation
rk2gkφ = rk∂u∂vφ + ∂urk∂vφ + ∂vrk∂uφ = 0.
(1.7)
By analogy with the bounds satisfied by the k-self-similar scalar field (1.6), we make the following
definition:
Definition 1.4. A sufficiently regular, spherically symmetric, solution φ(u, v) to (1.7) is said to satisfy
C1-self-similar bounds if the following pointwise bounds hold:

Remark 1.5. More generally, one can consider self-similar bounds for higher-order derivatives and for non-
spherically symmetric solutions. Given the finite H¨older regularity of the background solution, appropriate
modifications must be made in the definition for higher-order derivatives.
In the case of a single linear wave equation, the effect of the perturbation is reflected in the solution to
the wave equation under the perturbed initial data. Solutions φ which fail to satisfy C1-self-similar bounds
are indicative of a linear instability for the background spacetime, whereas those satisfying better-than-self-
similar bounds are indicative of linear asymptotic stability. Hence, we will be interested in the conditions
under which such bounds occur.

Blue-shift instability mechanism for BV data
In this section, we consider the case c0 ̸= 0, i.e., ∂vφ

We interpret this result as indicating that solutions are unstable for generic choices of outgoing data g0(v)
in function spaces where c0 can vary. In particular, since g0(v) vanishes identically in the interior region on
the initial hypersurface, ∂vφ has a jump discontinuity at (u, v) = (−1, 0). Consequently, the initial data for
∂vφ belongs to the function class of bounded variation. This instability is due to the geometry intrinsic to
the ingoing null-cone, and is termed the blue-shift instability. We refer to the particular instability rate
(1.13) as the blue-shift rate.
Higher-regularity stabilizing effect
The natural follow-up question is whether c0 = 0 is sufficient for
self-similar bounds to hold. Here, the H¨older regularity of g0(v), particularly the value of α, is decisive. We
only present heuristics here; a rigorous analysis is given in [10].

similarity coordinate z = v(−u)−1+k2 in a neighborhood of the singular horizon where 0 ≤z ≪1. For the

Finally, we close the discussion in this section of the exterior perturbations with the following three
remarks.
Remark 1.6. Although we only consider the characteristic problem (1.8) with the trivial initial data on the
ingoing cone, as shown in [9], one can also consider the non-trivial ingoing data ∂uφ = f0(u)
(−u) with some
additional decay assumption on f0(u). The heuristics and their conclusions provided in this section will
follow line by line.
Remark 1.7. The underlying mechanism for the exterior perturbations can be understood as a local ODE-
type mechanism. The nature of these perturbations provides a priori control of certain quantities in both
null directions, allowing both the blue-shift instability mechanism and high-regularity stabilizing effects to
be analyzed locally near the past light cone emanating from the singularity.
Remark 1.8. The analysis on the wave equation (1.8) suggests that the solution to the linear wave equation
is only orbitally stable for perturbations at the threshold. This aspect is further exploited in the nonlinear
analysis in [10] to show the orbital stability at the nonlinear level for exterior perturbations at the threshold.
However, using the fact that Einstein-scalar field equations are scaling invariant, one can in fact upgrade the
orbital exterior stability at the threshold to asymptotic stability; see Section 3 in the companion paper [12].
1.3.2
Problem 2: linear wave equation in the interior region
The heuristics for exterior perturbations rely essentially on a priori control in the ingoing direction. On the
one hand, the above approach is robust in the sense that, once such a priori control is available, it can be
applied to understand (in)stability of naked singularity solutions to Einstein equations coupled with other
matter models in the exterior region.
On the other hand, for interior perturbations—where no a priori
estimate in the ingoing direction is available—the above ODE-type approach fails to provide any insight into
the behavior of the interior perturbations. In this section, we provide a way to understand the blue-shift
mechanism and the high-regularity stabilizing effect for the interior perturbation.
We consider solutions in the interior region of mode-type
(rkφ)(u, v) = (−u)ρf(z),
(1.15)
where z = v/(−u)1−k2 ∈[−1, 0] is the self-similar coordinate. If ℜ(ρ) > 1, the mode solution satisfies a
bound better than the self-similar bound; if 1 −k2 < ℜ(ρ) < 1, the mode solution satisfies a bound between
the self-similar rate and the blue-shift rate; if ℜ(ρ) ≤1 −k2, the mode solution satisfies a bound worse
than the self-similar rate. As discussed in the previous section, any rate worse than the self-similar rate is
expected to correspond to the instability.
Then, for mode-type solutions, the wave equation (1.7) reduces to an ODE for the profile f(z)
(1 −k2)zf ′′(z) + (1 −k2 −ρ)f ′(z) + Vk(z)f(z) = 0.
(1.16)
Here, Vk(z) is a non-negative, H¨older continuous potential determined by the background metric. Since
rk = 0 at the center, the solution f(z) should satisfy the Dirichlet boundary condition, i.e., f(−1) = 0.

Although this mode perspective immediately gives instability at the level of the linear wave equation for
regularities below threshold, it leaves open the case of regularities above threshold, which is treated in [10].
However, it clarifies the essential difference between the two cases: whereas dynamics in the former case are
determined by a continuum of modes defined by the local behavior of solutions to (1.10) near {z = 0}, in the
latter the dynamics are determined by a discrete set of modes reflecting the global behavior of solutions to
(1.10). Mode stability in the high-regularity setting is therefore more subtle, necessitating the use of some
global properties of the potential Vk(z).
1.3.3
Difference between the linear wave equation and the linearized Einstein–scalar field
equations
Lastly, as pointed out in Section 1.2, the stability mechanism for the linear wave equation in the interior of
the k-self-similar naked singularity, discussed in Section 1.3.2, is not sufficient to fully capture the stability
mechanism for the linearized Einstein–scalar field equations, for the following two reasons.
The first issue concerns the regularity of the background scalar field. To illustrate this point, consider
mode-type solutions to (1.16). The rigorous construction of the corresponding outgoing solutions relies on the
regularity of Vk, which depends only on the background function rk and belongs to C1,k2/(1−k2). However,
if one formulates the linearized Einstein–scalar field equations as wave equations for r, ϕ, and Ω2, then
the mode-type solutions to the corresponding ODE system involve potential terms containing derivatives of
ϕk, which have only C0,k2/(1−k2) regularity. This loss of regularity obstructs the rigorous construction of
outgoing solutions; see Section 3.4.3 for details.
The second issue concerns the coupling between the background geometry and the background scalar
field. To rigorously implement the mechanism described in Section 1.3.2, the approach in [10] is to take the
Fourier–Laplace transform of the linear wave equation for ψ = rkφ and to analyze the scattering resolvent
and resonances of the resulting ODE. For the linear wave equation, there is only one scattering resonance,
corresponding to the translation invariance of the Einstein–scalar field equations. By contrast, due to the
coupling between the background geometry and the background scalar field, the analogous ODE system
arising from the linearized Einstein–scalar field equations may admit additional scattering resonances. Such
resonances would generate an additional instability mechanism and hence fall outside the framework of [10];
see Section 3.4.3 for details.
1.4
Overview of the paper
In Section 2, we recall some important properties of the k-self-similar naked singularities. In Section 3,
we introduce our main results and the proof outline. The analysis of the linearized Einstein-scalar field
equations will take the majority of this paper. Section 4 establishes the energy estimate for the linearized
system and obtains a non-sharp decay result. Section 5 establishes the scattering theory for the linearized
Einstein-scalar field operator. Sections 6–7 conclude the decay estimate for the linearized system.

2.1
Einstein-scalar field equations under spherical symmetry
The Einstein-scalar field equations are a coupled system for metric g and the scalar field ϕ, taking the form
of
Ric(g)µν = 2∂µϕ∂νϕ,
(2.1)
2gϕ = 0.
(2.2)
The solution to the Einstein-scalar field equations is a Lorentzian manifold M equipped with a Lorentzian
metric g and the associated scalar field ϕ : M →R. If the solution (M, g, ϕ) is invariant under the SO(3)
action, then we call the solution spherically symmetric. Let Q be the quotient manifold M/SO(3) and
p : M →Q be the projection map. Then p can naturally induce a quotient Lorentzian metric gQ on Q. The
set of fixed points under the SO(3) action is called the center or the axis of the spacetime and is denoted as
Γ.

The coordinate system in the original construction of the k-self-similar spacetime in [3] was under the so-
called Bondi coordinates.

Remark 2.5. We remark that the threshold in this context refers to the regularity of the scalar field in the
background k-self-similar spacetime. Recall that the geometric quantity rk is in the function space C2−
N
and
is C2,k2pk. Consequently, in our choice of the initial interior perturbation for the linearized problem, the
regularity requirement of rp is already lower than that of the background geometry. Nevertheless, the results
of this paper show that the linearized stability of the k-self-similar naked singularity spacetime depends
crucially on the regularity of the scalar field perturbation, while being largely insensitive to the regularity of
rp.
Remark 2.6. Due to the constraint equations of the Einstein-scalar field equation under the spherically
symmetric assumption, the regularity of r is closely related to the regularity of the scalar field ϕ.
In
particular, in the nonlinear problem, once the initial regularity of ϕ is given, the regularity of both ϕ and
r is consequently fixed.
Therefore, one should distinguish the regularity of the nonlinear problem and
the freedom of imposing r0
p in the linearized problem. The aforementioned regularity freedom of r0
p in the
linearized problem should be interpreted as follows: in estimating the decay rate of solutions to the linearized
Einstein-scalar field equations, only the Cα′
N -norm of rp enters the analysis. This does not imply that, in the
nonlinear problem, the regularity of rp can be below C2,k2pk.

3.2
Trivial mode from the translation invariance
To provide sufficient motivation for our proof outline, in this section, we address one of the main difficulties
in the proof, namely, the trivial mode generated by the translation invariance of the Einstein-scalar field
equations.
Recall that under the global double-null coordinates (u, v) introduced in Section 2, the interior of the k-
self-similar naked singularity solution (rk, mk, ϕk) to the spherically symmetric Einstein-scalar field equations
has the following behaviors when approaching the singularity (0, 0) for 0 ≤i + j ≤1:
∂i
u∂j
vrk ≈(−u)1−i−qkj,

Let (r, m, ϕ) be the solution of the perturbed initial data and (rp, mp, ϕp) := (r −rk, m −mk, ϕ −ϕk) be
the collection of the perturbed quantities. A naive approach, which has been widely employed in the study
of nonlinear wave equations, is to construct a suitable vector field and then use the energy method together
with Sobolev embedding to show that the perturbed quantities (rp, mp, ϕp) behave better than those of the
k-self-similar solution. In particular, one has the following expectation

However, since the wave equation for ϕ (2.7) degenerates at the center r = 0, to avoid this degeneracy,
it is convenient to work with ψ = rϕ instead of ϕ. Then the expectation for ψp becomes
∂i

As we have discussed extensively in Section 2, the spherically symmetric Einstein-scalar field equations
exhibit two important symmetries: the scaling symmetry and the translation symmetry. These symmetries,
on the one hand, play a crucial role in the construction of the k-self-similar naked singularities in [3]. On
the other hand, they create a severe obstacle to demonstrating the nonlinear stability under perturbations
above the threshold. To address this, under the (r, m, ψ)-formulation of solutions to the Einstein-scalar-field
equations, if (r, m, ψ) is a solution, then so is (r, m, ψ + cr) for any c ∈R. Although these two solutions are
essentially the same up to a translation, they correspond to different initial data for ψ, differing by a multiple
of r. By our gauge choice r|Σ−1 = rk ∈C2 on the initial hypersurface, the regularity of this difference lies
above the threshold. Consequently, the regularity constraint on our initial perturbation cannot detect this
trivial mode generated by the translation symmetry. Since r only behaves like
∂i

∂i

vψp
 ≲(−u)1−i−qkj.
Put it differently, the trivial mode r = rk + rp generated by the translation invariance prevents us from
obtaining a sharper bound for ψp. Consequently, even for arbitrarily small perturbations of regularity above
the threshold, establishing improved behavior for ψp requires a method to exclude this trivial mode:
∂i

3.3
A recap of the study on a linear wave equation
In fact, even at the level of a linear wave equation on the k-self-similar naked singularity spacetime, the
same difficulty of excluding the trivial mode presents. If one considers the wave equation for the quantity
ψ = rkϕ, the linear wave equation will be reduced to
∂s∂zψ + qkz∂2

Since ϕ = const is always a solution to the linear wave equation, the equation (3.11) admits a trivial solution

In the work [10], Singh carefully analyzed the behavior of the solution to the linear wave equation and
provided a scattering approach to exclude the trivial mode. Since the approach in the present paper is closely
related to the work of the first author [10], we give a detailed review here.

Note that the center Γ corresponds to the curve x = 0 and the singularity horizon {v = 0} corresponds to

To translate the above characteristic initial value problem to a standard Cauchy data formulation of a
wave equation in the one-dimensional scattering theory, we first give free data on the characteristic hyper-
surface {v = 0, u ≤−1} and propagate the solution backwards. By choosing the free data properly, one can
draw a connection between the regularity of the initial perturbation and the asymptotic tail on the Cauchy
hypersurface {t = 0}:

as two transport equations in two characteristic directions to get pointwise asymptotic bounds. However,
this step is not robust when considering the linearized Einstein-scalar field equations around
the k-self-similar naked singularity spacetime.

Step 2: Scattering theory
We consider the equation (3.11) with prescribed initial data on {s = 0, −1 ≤
z ≤0} in the function space Cα with α > pk. Since the solution is only defined on the upper half plane
s ≥0, to extend the solution to the lower half plane and use the standard theory of the Fourier–Laplace
transform, we commute the equation (3.11) with a cut-off function supported on s ≥1. Then the problem
is reduced to the following inhomogeneous problem
∂s∂zψ + qkz∂2
zψ + qk∂zψ + Vk(z)ψ = ∂zF,

s ≤0.
The forcing term F is determined by the truncation function and the initial data. In particular, we have
that F ∈Cα is compactly supported in s.
Taking the Fourier–Laplace transform, the problem is further reduced to studying the operator:

3.4
Analysis on the linearized operator
As mentioned before, a key step in proving the nonlinear stability is a careful analysis of the linearized
Einstein-scalar field equations, denoted by P(rp, Ψp, mp) = 0. We follow the same framework as in [10]. In
this section, we point out the main difficulties and their resolutions.
3.4.1
Linear analysis for perturbations at the threshold
One of the advantages of using (r, Ψ, m) formulation is that all three quantities respect the scaling invariance
of the Einstein-scalar field equations, i.e., if (r, Ψ, m) is a solution, then so is (ar, aΨ, am) for any a ∈R. This
invariance naturally induces a non-trivial kernel for the linearized operator P, namely P(rk, Ψk, mk) = 0.
Therefore, for the linearized Einstein-scalar field equations with initial data at the threshold, using Lemma
2.4 and subtracting the kernel arising from the scaling invariance, we can reduce the problem to perturbations
above the threshold. Therefore, in the following sections, we focus on perturbations above the threshold.

3.4.2
The role of the energy estimate
Although we have mentioned that it is impossible to prove a good upper bound for Ψ by only using the
energy estimate without excluding the trivial mode solution in Section 3.2, the vector field method still plays
a role here.
First, even at the level of the linear wave equation, the scattering approach emphasized in the previous
section can only prove a better bound in the near-axis region. To extend the result to the entire interior
region, one has to appeal to the energy estimate. The same situation also appears for the linearized Einstein-
scalar field equations.
Second, applying the inverse Fourier–Laplace transform requires an a priori L∞-estimate for the solution.
For the linear wave equation, this a priori estimate can be obtained without invoking any energy estimates,
since the contour lies in the right half-plane. In the linearized Einstein-scalar field equations, however, some
fake modes appear in the right half-plane; see Section 3.4.3 for an overview of the presence of these fake
modes. Using the vector field method and Sobolev embedding, we obtain some non-trivial decay (Theorem
4.1), allowing the contour to be placed in the left half-plane and avoiding the fake modes.
In the energy estimate, although the vector ∂s in the self-similar coordinates corresponds to the natural
conformal Killing vector field K in the k-self-similar naked singularity spacetime, it turns out that ∂s is not
useful in obtaining energy estimates. In fact, we use the null vector field ∂z extensively.
Our basic strategy in the energy estimate is to construct suitable multipliers for the wave equations for
rp and Ψp. For mp, we always use the z-transport equation (2.29) of mp to control the L2 norm of mp(s, ·)
on z ∈[−1, 0] in terms of the L2-norm of rp and Ψp.
To close the first-order energy estimate for rp and Ψp, one has to control the L2-norm of
mp

first-order derivatives of rp and Ψp. However, near the axis, the quantity
mp

3.4.3
Main difficulty in the scattering theory for the linearized Einstein-scalar field equations
Besides the technical difficulties arising in the energy estimates, additional substantial obstacles emerge in
the scattering analysis of the linearized Einstein-scalar field equations.
Problematic ∂vϕk term
The linear wave equation (3.11) on the k-self-similar metric is only a toy model
for the nonlinear problem or the linearized problem, since it does not capture the most dangerous behavior
generated by ∂vϕk in the k-self-similar naked singularity spacetime. The presence of ∂vϕk is problematic for
two reasons:
24


## Page 25


• (Loss of global smallness) In the linear wave equation (3.11), due to the absence of ∂vϕk and the
presence of µk, the potential term Vk is a globally small quantity. This global smallness is used in an
essential way in [10] to determine the location of the scattering resonance (see Remark 3.3). However,
as we have mentioned in Section 2.2, ∂vϕk behaves like 1
k near the singular horizon, which is a large
quantity if we take k to be sufficiently small.
• (Bad regularity) As opposed to Remark 3.4, the quantity ∂vϕk has only C0,pkk2 regularity, which
generates a bad sub-leading term in the expansion.
Consequently, this lack of enough regularity
creates serious difficulties in constructing outgoing and ingoing solutions.
Non-trivial coupling between the geometry and the scalar field
In the a priori energy estimates,
the difficulties do not arise from the number of equations. That is, although we deal with two wave equations,
once we understand how to do the energy estimate for the wave equation for rp, the corresponding energy
estimate for Ψp will follow similarly. Surprisingly, in the analysis of the scattering resonance, the presence of
coupled multiple equations introduces additional challenges. Similar to the analysis in Section 3.3, the trivial
solution arising from the translation symmetry to the linearized Einstein-scalar field equations (rp, Ψp, mp) =
(0, rk, 0) will give scattering resonance at σ = −1. To exclude other scattering resonances, heuristically, in
the construction of the outgoing solution to (3.17), the renormalization factor 1 +
σ
qk in the recurrence
relation (3.18) suggests that σ = −1 should be the only scattering resonance.

## Page 26


there are essentially two scattering resonances, which may or may not be located at the same point; in the
former case, this corresponds to a scattering resonance of multiplicity two. In the context of the linearized
Einstein-scalar field equations, which are a system of coupled wave equations, the corresponding difficulty is
expected to arise from the nontrivial coupling between the geometric quantity r and the scalar field ϕ. On
the one hand, if stability holds, one expects this system of wave equations to admit only one single scattering
resonance from the translation symmetry of the Einstein-scalar field equations. On the other hand, the toy
model suggests a possible additional instability mechanism arising from the interaction between
the geometry and the scalar field, resulting in the presence of multiple scattering resonances.
3.4.4
Formulation of the scattering analysis
In this section, we will state the formulation of the scattering analysis in terms of the self-similar coordinates
(s, z); see (5.2)-(5.4) for the precise equations. Under the self-similar coordinates (s, z), the trivial mode
from the translation invariance will be σ = −1 and the domain of interest of σ will be ℜσ > −3
2.
Under the (rp, Ψp, mp)-formulation of the linearized Einstein-scalar field equations, we need to deal with
the wave equation for rp, the wave equation for Ψp, and two transport equations for mp. To apply the
framework in [10], it is natural to derive a wave equation for mp from those two transport equations for
mp (2.9)–(2.10). The resulting wave equation for mp will take the form of
∂u∂vmp + · · · =
rk
∂vrk
∂umk∂vϕk∂vϕp + · · · .
However, this equation will lead to severe complications because ∂vϕk is present in the scattering analysis,
in view of the discussion in Section 3.4.3, Remark 3.3, and Remark 3.4.
An explanation for why this complication happens is that by only looking at three wave equations, one
cannot retrieve the original important information carried by the constraint equations. Hence, we keep the
original two wave equations and two transport equations in the formulation and directly apply the Fourier–
Laplace transform. It turns out that the ∂s-transport equation for mp, after the Fourier–Laplace transform,
will be an algebraic equation in the following form:
(σ + Terms on the background) ˆmp =(· · · )z∂zϕk∂z ˆΨp + (· · · )z∂zϕk∂zˆrp + (· · · )z∂zϕk ˆΨp + (· · · )z∂zϕkˆrp
+ Other terms only involving ˆrp and ˆΨp without ∂zϕk,
(3.20)
where · · · represents terms depending on the background that do not involve ϕk. We can directly express
ˆmp by ˆrp and ˆΨp from this algebraic equation and then substitute the ˆmp terms in differential equations
for ˆrp and ˆΨp by this expression. The coefficient before ˆmp in the above algebraic equation will be zero
for some points on the right half-plane of σ. We call these points fake modes, and they will be excluded
by restricting our domain of interest in the left half-plane of σ. This aspect is different from the work [10],
where no fake mode appears and the inverse Fourier–Laplace transform is performed in the upper half-plane.
The consequence of this formulation is that we only need to analyze the differential equations for ˆrp and ˆΨp
in the scattering analysis.
3.4.5
Resolution to the presence of ∂vϕk
In view of the discussion in Section 3.3 on the linear wave equation, Remark 3.3, and Remark 3.4, the
presence of the problematic ∂vϕk terms essentially obstructs the construction of the outgoing and ingoing
solutions to the corresponding ODE system after taking the Fourier–Laplace transform. For convenience,
we focus only on the outgoing solutions here. The way to resolve the difficulty caused by the ∂vϕk terms in
the construction of outgoing solutions relies on the following three observations.
Observation 1: good regularity in the ∂s-direction
By the nature of the k-self-similar naked singu-
larity spacetime (Proposition 2.1), the ∂v derivative, or equivalently the ∂z derivative, should be regarded
as a bad derivative, due to the 1
k-largeness of ∂vϕk and its poor regularity on the singular horizon v = 0.
However, since u∂u agrees with the conformal Killing vector field
K = u∂u + v∂v = ∂s
26


## Page 27


of (Mk, gk, ϕk) on the singular horizon, the quantity u∂uϕk is in fact smooth there and enjoys k-smallness,
by the k-self-similarity with 0 < k2 ≪1. Therefore, the vector field ∂u in double-null coordinates and the
conformal Killing vector field ∂s in self-similar coordinates should be viewed as good derivatives. This is
analogous to the standard theory of linear wave equations, where vector fields transverse to the light cone
are regarded as bad derivatives.
Observation 2: advantage of the formulation in Section 3.4.4
In the formulation of the scattering
analysis introduced in Section 3.4.4, the differential equations for ˆrp and ˆΨp; see (5.2)–(5.3), do not contain
terms involving ∂vϕk directly. The ∂vϕk terms enter the system only through the equation for ˆmp (5.4).
However, this equation is obtained by taking the Fourier–Laplace transform of ∂smp, which is a good
derivative of mp in the sense of Observation 1. Thus, this formulation turns out to overcome the difficulty
caused by the problematic ∂vϕk terms. Indeed, by Proposition 2.1, in self-similar coordinates we have

Combining the ideas from the above three observations and carefully running the Volterra iteration for
constructing outgoing solutions, we can construct the desired outgoing and ingoing solutions to the linearized
Einstein–scalar field operator; see Section 5.5. Finally, we conclude that the resolution of the difficulty caused
by the problematic ∂vϕk terms ultimately relies on the favorable ∂s-regularity of the background k-self-similar
spacetime. Beyond the technical observations in this section, the analysis of the scattering resolvent and
resonances is naturally associated with the ∂s vector field in self-similar coordinates, with respect to which
the background quantities exhibit good regularity.
3.4.6
Resolution to the coupling between the geometry and the scalar field: cancellation from
the expansion at the singular horizon
The second difficulty from the interaction between the geometric quantity r and the scalar field ϕ in the
scattering analysis can be resolved by a cancellation from the expansion of the k-self-similar naked singularity
spacetime at the singular horizon.
27


## Page 28


For a system of decoupled equations, such as the toy model problem (3.19), one cannot avoid the presence
of multiple scattering resonances, since each equation directly contributes a scattering resonance. However,
for a system of coupled equations with suitable coefficients, cancellations may occur, so that only a single
scattering resonance remains.
To illustrate this, we consider the following toy model problem with two
coupled equations:
qkz d2f
dz2 + (1 + σ) df
dz + (1 + σ)k2g + (1 + σ)k2f = 0,
qkz d2g
dz2 + (qk + σ)dg
dz + αk2f = 0.
(3.22)
Assume that the corresponding outgoing solutions take the form of

An(−z)n.
Plugging the above expressions into (3.22), we can get the following analogous recurrence relations as in (3.18)
n(qkn + k2 + σ)an = (1 + σ)k2An−1 + (1 + σ)k2an−1,
n(qkn + σ)An = αk2an−1.
Therefore, each outgoing solution can be determined if one specifies the values of a0 and A0. Just as in (3.18),
to make each an and An holomorphic functions of σ for ℜσ > −3
2, one has to renormalize the values of a0
and A0. In this toy model problem, a solution basis of the outgoing solutions can be obtained by choosing
(a0, A0) = (1 + σ, 0) and (a0, A0) = (0, 1), respectively.
One can easily check that for these choices of
(a0, A0), the resulting sequence (an, An) depends holomorphically on σ when ℜσ > −3
2. Moreover, as we
have discussed in Section 3.3, this suggests that there is only one scattering resonance for this system. We
remark that the choices of (a0, A0) in this toy model problem crucially rely on the coefficients in (3.22).
Now, let us go back to the linearized Einstein-scalar field equations.
Using the formulation of the
scattering analysis mentioned above, we only need to deal with two coupled differential equations for ˆrp and
ˆΨp. In the study of the linear wave [10], the explicit expansion of Vk does not matter, i.e., we do not need
the explicit value of ωk in the expansion (3.12). However, to deal with the danger of producing multiple
scattering resonances arising from the coupling of r and ϕ, we expand various gauge invariant quantities
that appear in the equations (5.2)-(5.4) at the singular horizon {z = 0} and compute the leading order
coefficients explicitly.
Then a cancellation analogous to that in (3.22) occurs.
In fact, this cancellation
removes the need to introduce a renormalization factor in the construction of one of the outgoing solutions,
thereby eliminating the aforementioned potential instability mechanism; see Proposition 5.14. Therefore,
we conclude the presence of exactly one scattering resonance σ = −1 for the linearized Einstein-scalar field
equations in Section 5.8
4
Multiplier estimates for the linearized equations
In this section, we will establish the non-sharp decay result for the solution (Ψp, rp, mp) to the linearized
Einstein-scalar field equations P(rp, Ψp, mp) = 0 using energy estimates.
Precisely, we will prove the following theorem.
Theorem 4.1 (Non-sharp decay estimate). Let (rp, Ψp, mp) be the solution to the linearized equations

To prove Theorem 4.1, we apply the energy estimates to the equations (2.26)-(2.29) under the self-similar
coordinates (s, z). Let
Ψρ = e(qk−ρ)sΨp,
rρ = e(qk−ρ)srp,
mρ = e(qk−ρ)smp.
(4.7)
The desired decay rate in Theorem 4.1 will follow from the boundedness of (rρ, Ψρ, mρ) for some suitable
choice of ρ, while the boundedness of (rρ, Ψρ, mρ) can be established by the standard multiplier estimates
and the Sobolev embedding. One should note that the smaller values of ρ will correspond to a sharper decay.
Remark 4.2. Under the change of variables (4.7), assuming the boundedness of (rρ, Ψρ, mρ) and their deriva-
tives, the case ρ = 0 corresponds to the blue-shift decay rate of Ψp; the case ρ > 0 corresponds to a decay
rate slower than the blue-shift rate for Ψp; the case −k2 < ρ < 0 corresponds to a decay rate between the
self-similar rate and the blue-shift rate for Ψp; and ρ < −k2 corresponds to a rate faster than the self-similar
rate.
Remark 4.3. If the stability of the k-self-similar spacetime is expected under the high-regularity perturba-
tions, one may ask whether there exists an energy approach that pushes the value of ρ below the threshold
ρ = −k2, thereby yielding a (linearized) stability result directly. We explain why the decay rate in Theorem
4.1 is not sharp and cannot be made sharp solely by energy estimates.
The Einstein-scalar field equa-
tions are invariant under the translation (r, ϕ, m) →(r, ϕ + c, m). Accordingly, the linearized equations
P(rp, Ψp, mp) = 0 are also invariant under the translation (rp, Ψp, mp) →(rp, Ψp + crk, mp). From the
viewpoint of the evolution problem, this translation changes the initial data (rp, Ψp)

Σ−1 by a multiple of
rk, which will not affect the regularity class of the initial data since rk is at least C2. In other words, the
regularity restriction of the initial data alone can not distinguish two essentially equivalent sets of initial data
modulo this translation. Moreover, in self-similar coordinates, rk decays only at the rate e−s. For general
high-regularity perturbations, observing improved decay with ρ < −k2 therefore requires considering the
renormalized quantity (rp, Ψp −crk, mp) for a suitable choice of c. Hence, without a priori knowledge of the
correct value of c, one cannot expect to close the energy estimate for (rρ, Ψρ, mρ) when ρ < −k2.

4.6
A quick overview of the second-order energy estimate
As we can see from (4.48), the first order energy estimate itself is not enough to even get the L2-boundedness
of the solutions. The main obstacle here is the presence of the bad sign of the bulk term when ρ is sufficiently
close to the blue-shift value. To trace the origin of the bad sign, expressing the principal part of the wave
operator ∂u∂v under the self-similar coordinates (s, z) and using (rρ, Ψρ) as variables, we can schematically
write the equations (4.10)-(4.11) as
∂s∂zΞ + qkz∂2
zΞ + ρ∂zΞ = Error terms,
Ξ ∈{rρ, Ψρ}.
(4.57)
Multiplying by ∂zΞ and applying integration by parts, we can derive:

To get as sharp a decay rate as possible, the value of ρ shall be chosen to be at least close to the blue-shift
value, thus creating the bad sign in the coefficients of the bulk terms in the first-order energy estimates.
Nonetheless, this bad sign can be compensated for by the second-order energy estimate. Commuting the
equation (4.57) with ∂z, we can derive
∂s∂2
zΞ + qkz∂3

where we have gained qk in the coefficient of the terms with second-order z-derivative. However, due to the
low-regularity nature of Ψρ, we have to add a suitable z-weight in the choice of the multiplier. A natural
choice of the multiplier might be (−z)2γ∂2
zΨρ, where γ ∈[0, 1
2) depending on the regularity of Ψρ. If Ψρ ∈Cα
for α ∈(1, 2), one can roughly view Ψρ as (−z)α. Then the correct weight γ should be 3

arbitrarily close to 0. Multiplying (−z)2γ∂2

If the regularity of Ψρ is sufficiently close to the threshold, i.e., α →pk, then the coefficient for the bulk

will only be positive for ρ > −k2. Recall that ρ = −k2 corresponds to the self-similar value ρ. Hence, only
in view of this bulk term, second-order energy may be closed for ρ arbitrarily close to the self-similar value.
However, the estimate for the error terms will generate several bulk terms with coefficients of size k on the
left-hand side of (4.58), which can only be absorbed by the bulk term on the right-hand side of (4.58) if
ρ > Bk for some constant B.
One key novelty of our second-order energy estimate is that, instead of using (−z)2γ∂2

4.10
Some further remarks

have positive bulk terms for any ρ > −qk(α −1). In particular, when α > pk, this allows the value of ρ to
be smaller than the self-similar value ρ = −k2. However, to prove the decay estimate, due to the presence

Remark 5.1. The above equations can be obtained informally by taking the Fourier–Laplace transform (5.1)
of equations (2.26)-(2.29) and applying integration by parts with the assumption that (rp, Ψp, mp)|s≤0 =
0. However, since the initial data (rp, Ψp, mp)|s=0 is indeed non-trivial, in reality, we shall commute the
equations (2.26)-(2.29) with a cut-off function supported away from s = 0 first. This procedure will generate
some inhomogeneous terms on the right-hand side of (5.2)-(5.4). The corresponding equations will be studied
in Section 6. In this section, we will only focus on the linear homogeneous equations (5.2)-(5.4).

One can directly apply the same argument to construct the outgoing solutions to (5.16)-(5.17). However,
note that the coefficient before a1 in the algebraic equation (5.23) will degenerate when σ = −1. Similarly, the

following proposition constructing the outgoing solutions for σ ∈I[−1−η,−η]\{−1, −qk}:

Remark 5.12. The above proposition will not play a role in our proof of the main result of this paper. We
state the proposition to highlight the key role played by the special structure of the background k-self-similar
spacetime. Without some additional structures, the construction of the outgoing solution will just fail at
σ = −1, −qk.
Remark 5.13. Note that in the above proposition, we did not track the σ-dependence of the estimate (5.38).
However, since `an,σ, `An,σ, ´an,σ, ´An,σ have poles at σ = −1, −qk, the σ-dependence will become singular at
those points.

5.3
Construction of outgoing solutions to Lp = 0
As mentioned in the previous section, for a general system of ODEs (5.16)-(5.17), in the region I[−1−η,−η],
while the construction of ingoing solutions holds true in the whole region I[−1−η,−η], the construction of
outgoing solutions is only valid for σ away from σ = −1, −qk, due to the degeneracy of some coefficients.
To avoid the presence of this degeneracy, one approach is to renormalize the values of a0 and A0 to be
a0 = A0 = (1 + σ)

at σ = −1. Hence, the only renormalization arises from the degeneracy of the coefficients at σ = −qk. More

Remark 5.15. The equation (5.46) can be reduced to L = 0 by moving all the zeroth-order terms on the
left-hand side of (5.7)-(5.8) to the right-hand side of (5.7)-(5.8) and defining E1,σ and E2,σ accordingly.
However, since the ingoing solutions are insensitive to the precise structure of the equations, we provide a
general framework here.
Remark 5.16. Note that E1 and E2 are not necessarily analytic. Hence, one can not directly apply the stan-
dard theory of constructing outgoing and ingoing solutions for a system of ODEs with regular singularities.
In fact, since the coefficients on the right-hand side of (5.7)-(5.8) are from the k-self-similar naked singularity
spacetime, E1 and E2 should be thought of as H¨older-continuous matrix-valued functions.
We adopt the following convention:
Convention 5.17. Let N be a n × m matrix. We denote by Nij the element of N in the i-th row and j-th
column. For m = 1, we abbreviate the notation by writing Ni the element of N in the i-th row. For m = n,
we denote the co-factor of Nij as [N]ij.

Remark 5.21. In fact, one can obtain much more refined information on the ingoing solutions using the
approach in Section 5.5. However, in the later analysis of the scattering resonance, it suffices to know the
asymptotic behavior of the ingoing solutions. Therefore, we only provide crude estimates on the ingoing
solutions in this section.
5.5
Construction of outgoing solutions for the linearized system and its expan-
sions
Now we are ready to construct outgoing solutions to L(ˆrp, ˆΨp) = 0. Since we can write the equation L = 0
in the form of (5.46) , the proof in Proposition 5.18 might also be used to construct outgoing solutions.
However, the proof of Proposition 5.18 has two limitations. First, the argument in Proposition 5.18 relies on
the fact that A(0) = B(0) = 0, while for the outgoing solutions, due to the presence of non-trivial values of
A(0) and B(0), the same approach will fail to give a convergent Volterra sequence. Second, we need to track
the k-smallness and σ-dependence of the expansion of the outgoing solutions while the proof of Proposition
5.18 fails to give a detailed information on the (k, σ)-dependence.
To conclude, the construction of the outgoing solutions has three challenges
57


## Page 58


• The existence of the outgoing solutions in the whole region I[−1−η,−η].
• The k-smallness in the expansion of the outgoing solutions.
• The σ-boundedness in the expansion of the outgoing solutions.
In this section, we exploit the extra structures
dˆrp

appearing on the right-hand side of the
equation (5.15) to construct the outgoing solutions and track the leading order behaviors and k-smallness
of the sub-leading terms. Moreover, we will achieve the uniform σ-boundedness of the coefficients in the
expansions.

5.5.1
Analysis on the fundamental matrix Yσ
To facilitate our later analysis, we first analyze the fundamental matrix Yσ. Recall that in the construction
of Ψ+,σ, we need to renormalize the value of (a0, A0) to be (0, 1 + σ
qk ) to avoid the poles for the coefficients
in the expansion. However, this renormalization will result in the degeneracy of the coefficient of the leading
order term of Ψ+,σ when σ = −qk. The following lemma illustrates the consequence of this degeneracy.
Lemma 5.22. The construction of r+,σ and Ψ+,σ can be extended holomorphically to the whole region
σ ∈I[−1−η,−η]. When σ = −qk, Ψ+,σ is linearly dependent on Ψ−,σ.
Proof. The holomorphic extension follows from the fact that in the expansions of r+,σ and Ψ+,σ, the coeffi-
cients ¯an,σ, ¯An,σ, ean,σ, eAn,σ are holomorphic functions for σ ∈I[−1−η,−η]. For σ = −qk, we can compare the
algebraic relations (5.23) and (5.24) with (5.25) and (5.26):
(qkn + k2)(n + 1)an+1 = α1an + α2An,
qkn(n + 1)An+1 + k(n + 1)an+1 = α3An,
(n + 2)(qkn + 1)bn+1 = α1bn + α2Bn,
(n + 2)(qkn + qk)Bn+1 + k(n + 2)bn+1 = α3Bn
Note that the equations for an+1 and An+1 take the same forms as that for bn and Bn when σ = −qk,
respectively. Hence, we can conclude that when σ = −qk, Ψ+,σ is linearly dependent to Ψ−,σ.

5.6

In this section, we construct the Dirichlet solutions to the equation (5.46). First, we have the following

the Dirichlet solution gdir,σ is the unique solution to (5.46) with the boundary condition
(f, g, f ′, g′)|z=−1 = (0, 0, 0, 1).

5.8
Location of the scattering resonance
In this section, we consider the equation W(σ, −1) = 0, the solution of which corresponds to the scattering
resonance. First, we show that for σ ∈I[−1−η,−η] ∩{|ℑσ| ≥1}, W(σ, −1) can never be zero.

Now, we determine the exact location of the zero of W(σ, −1). We need two more preliminaries.
First, by our construction of the outgoing and ingoing solutions to the equation L(ˆrp, ˆΨp) = 0, any
solution (ˆrp, ˆΨp) will be a linear combination of ˆrout,σ, ˆΨout,σ, ˆrin,σ, ˆΨin,σ. In particular, near z = 0, the
asymptotic behavior of (ˆrp, ˆΨp) will be given by the linear combination of the asymptotic behaviors of the
outgoing and ingoing solutions. More precisely, the asymptotic behavior of (ˆrp, ˆΨp) will be
ˆrp ≈A1 + B1(−z)−σ
qk + C1(−z)−k2+σ
qk ,
ˆΨp ≈A2 + B2(−z)−σ
qk + C2(−z)−k2+σ
qk .
Since for σ ∈I[−1−η,−η], if A1 ̸= 0, A2 ̸= 0, the asymptotic behavior of the outgoing branch will be the leading
order. In other words, it is not enough to tell whether a solution (ˆrp, ˆΨp) to the equation L(ˆrp, ˆΨp) = 0
is outgoing or not by only looking at the leading order asymptotic behavior at z = 0. In the analysis of
the scattering resonance, to show W(σ, −1) = 0 for some σ, our approach is to construct a special solution
to L(ˆrp, ˆΨp) = 0 that satisfies the Dirichlet boundary condition at z = −1 and the outgoing boundary
condition at z = 0. In view of this purpose, we shall establish a sharp criterion for functions (f, g) to satisfy
the outgoing boundary condition. Therefore, we provide the following lemma, which connects the outgoing
solution with its regularity on the outgoing cone {u = −1} under self-similar coordinates.
Lemma 5.36 (Regularity perspective on outgoing boundary conditions). Fix σ ∈I[−1−η,−η]\{−qk}. Then
(ˆrp, ˆΨp) satisfies the outgoing boundary condition if and only if (ˆrp, ˆΨp) ∈Cα
z up to z = 0, where α is the
smallest integer larger than −σ+k2
qk .

The
following lemma provides a family of special solutions we will need.
Lemma 5.37. For any c ∈R, (rp, Ψp, mp) = (0, crk, 0) is a special solution to the linearized Einstein-scalar
field equations (2.22)-(2.25).
Proof. We observe that the Einstein-scalar field equations (2.3)-(2.7) are translation invariant. More pre-
cisely, since only the derivatives of ϕ appear in the equations, if (r, ϕ, m) is a solution, then for any
c ∈R, (r, ϕ + c, m) is also a solution.
Thus, in the (rp, ϕp, mp)-formulation of the linearized system,
(rp, ϕp, mp) = (0, c, 0) is a special solution. Since Ψp = rkϕp + Ψk
rk rp, we have (rp, Ψp, mp) = (0, crk, 0)
is a special solution to the linearized equations (2.22)-(2.25).

6.1

Moreover, we assume that the solution (rp, Ψp, mp) to (6.1) has the following decay rate under the

Remark 6.1. The motivations for studying the inhomogeneous problem (6.1) are as follows. First, when
studying the linearized Einstein-scalar field equations with non-trivial initial data, to facilitate the Fourier–
Laplace transform, we will truncate the solution to be supported away from {s = 0}. This truncation will
produce some inhomogeneous terms. One should think of the truncation function as only a function of s.
Hence, the truncation procedure will only produce some inhomogeneous terms for (P(1), P(2), P(3)). Second,
in the final study of the nonlinear stability, we will write the Einstein-scalar field equations as
Linearized part = Nonlinearity
and use the fixed point argument to prove some decay rates for the solutions. In the fixed point argument,
we will treat the nonlinearity as some known functions, which motivates the study of the inhomogeneous
problem (6.1).
6.2
Freedom of the inhomogeneous terms
However, since the inhomogeneous P = I originates from the nonlinear Einstein-scalar field equations, to
have well-posedness, we lose some freedom to choose the inhomogeneous functions I = (∂zI1, ∂zI2, I3, 0). In
this section, we discuss one formulation for the inhomogeneous terms.
Due to the v-transport equation (2.25) (equivalently (2.29)), integrating from the center in the v-direction,
we can write m as a function of r and Ψ. Formally, we have that
m = m[r, Ψ],
µ = µ[r, Ψ].
Hence, schematically we can write the Einstein-scalar field equations as
∂u∂vr =
µ[r, Ψ]

Hence, we can only choose the inhomogeneous terms I1 and I2 freely and, depending on the exact formulation
of the problem, I3 will be determined accordingly once we fix (I1, I2). For now, we use the word “compatible”
to refer to any reasonable inhomogeneous terms.

Step 3: Near-axis decay Now, we can apply Proposition 6.4 since the inhomogeneous terms satisfy
the assumption (6.3) and the solution (ξ(s)rp, ξ(s)Ψp, ξ(s)mp) satisfies the decay rate (6.4). Hence, we can
close the proof of Theorem 3.2 in the near-axis region.
Step 4: Decay in the whole region Finally, note that the decay rates in the near-axis region satisfy
the assumption (4.97). Hence, using Proposition 6.4 and Proposition 4.16, we can conclude the proof of
Theorem 3.2.

scalar field. Ann. Math., 140(3):607–653, 1994.

ematical Society, 2019.
